The ∞ \infty -category of ∞ \infty -categories in simplicial type theory
Daniel Gratzer, Jonathan Weinberger, Ulrik Buchholtz

Abstract
. Simplicial type theory ( STT ) was introduced by Riehl and Shulman to leverage homotopy type theory to prove results about ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -categories. Initial work on simplicial type theory focused on “formal” arguments in higher category theory and, in particular, no non-trivial examples of ∞ \infty -category theory were constructible within STT . More recent work has changed this state of affairs by applying techniques developed initially for cubical type theory to construct the ∞ \infty -category of spaces. We complete this process by constructing the ∞ \infty -category of ∞ \infty -categories, recovering one of the main foundational results of ∞ \infty -category theory (straightening–unstraightening) purely type-theoretically. We also show how this construction enables new examples of the directed version of the structure identity principle: the structure homomorphism principle.



Acknowledgements.

Jonathan Weinberger is grateful to the Fowler of School of Engineering at Chapman University for generous support of this work. He is particularly thankful to the Fletcher Jones Foundation and their award of a Fletcher Jones Foundation Faculty Fellowship in Engineering ’25–’28 and the ensuing generous funding of this work. He also thanks the Schmid School of Science and Technology as well as the Center of Excellence in Computation, Algebra, and Topology (CECAT), both at Chapman University, for providing an excellent research environment.

1.
Introduction

A defining characteristic of dependent type theories is their focus on universes of (small) types.
More than in other foundations of mathematics, such universes are critical for even proving such basic
properties as
0\neq 1

(
Smith 1989
)
. This focus on universes is only intensified with

homotopy type theory
(
HoTT
)
(
Univalent Foundations Program 2013
)
where the universe is supplemented with the
univalence axiom. Such
univalent
universes allow type theorists to view types as a synthetic
incarnation of spaces (i.e.,
\infty
-groupoids) with the intensional identity type
a=_{A}b

modeling paths in the type
A
. The utility of this viewpoint is demonstrated by development of
synthetic homotopy theory inside of
HoTT
: a reconstruction of classical results in homotopy theory
with simpler and more conceptual proofs.

A long-standing challenge in
HoTT
has been to broaden the reach of synthetic homotopy theory to
include homotopy-coherent algebraic structures and, especially, the homotopical enhancement of
category theory:
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\infty,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\infty,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\infty,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
-category theory.
1
1

1

In both the title and hereafter, we
shall simply write “
\infty
-category” or even “category” to refer to
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\infty,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\infty,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\infty,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
-categories. If
we wish to specifically discuss ordinary categories, we shall specifically denote them by 1-categories.
While
numerous approaches to this problem have been proposed
(
Licata and Harper 2011
;
Warren 2013
;
Nuyts 2015
;
Riehl and Shulman 2017
;
North 2018
;
Kavvos 2019
;
Nuyts 2020
;
Weaver and Licata 2020
;
Ahrens et al. 2023
;
Weaver 2024
;
Neumann and Altenkirch 2024
;
Neumann 2025b
)

we will focus on the approach
introduced by
Riehl and Shulman 2017
. There the authors leveraged a non-standard model
of
HoTT
where types are realized by simplicial spaces. In particular, they showed
that the complete Segal spaces—a known model of
\infty
-categories
(
Rezk 2001
)
—then
arise as certain types satisfying a pair of easily-defined properties. Thus, in this setting not
every type is an
\infty
-category, but every
\infty
-category gives rise to a valid type.

Concretely,
simplicial type theory
extends
HoTT
with a
directed interval
, a postulated
totally ordered lattice
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbb{I},0,1,\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbb{I},0,1,\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{I},0,1,\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{I},0,1,\leq$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. This new type is meant to represent the
\infty
-category
with two objects
0,1
and a single non-identity morphism
0\to 1
—an interpretation justified by
the model of
STT
in simplicial spaces—and we then use
\mathbb{I}
to define morphisms in an arbitrary
type
A
as ordinary functions
\mathbb{I}\to A
. By constraining the endpoints of a synthetic morphism,
we arrive at the definition of the space of synthetic morphisms in a type:

\hom_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{f:\mathbb{I}\to A}}{\textstyle\sum_{f:\mathbb{I}\to A}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{f:\mathbb{I}\to A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{f:\mathbb{I}\to A}}f\,0=a\times f\,1=b
.

Riehl and Shulman 2017
then demonstrate that the definition
of an
\infty
-category can be formulated concisely as a
predicate

\mathsf{isCat}
on types,
essentially requiring every pair of composable morphisms have a unique composite. Furthermore,
they show that ordinary functions between such types constitute functors and that other classical
definitions in
\infty
-category theory become expressible. Subsequent work has further expanded
this approach, developing fibered category theory
(
Buchholtz and Weinberger 2023
;
Weinberger 2022
)
, limits
and colimits
(
Bardomiano Martínez 2025b
)
, etc.

While not every type constitutes an
\infty
-category in
STT
, many type-theoretic operations
preserve the property of being an
\infty
-category. For instance,
\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{1}
are
the initial and terminal categories,
A\times B
(
A+B
) is the (co)product category, and
A\to B
is the category of functors. As an extension of
HoTT
,
STT
comes equipped with a (hierarchy of)
universes and it is therefore natural to ask:

Is
\mathcal{U}
a recognizable category, e.g., the category of categories?

Unfortunately, the answer is negative;
\mathcal{U}
is the canonical example of a type that is
not
an

\infty
-category in
STT
. In fact, even if one considers simple subtypes of the universe
(e.g.,
\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\textstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}\mathsf{isCat}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
) one does not obtain a category, as synthetic morphisms
\mathbb{I}\to\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\textstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}\mathsf{isCat}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
neither compose nor faithfully represent functors. However, it has
long been conjectured that the category of categories should be constructible in
STT
as a certain
subtype of the universe.

We address this final gap in the foundations of
STT
by settling this conjecture
affirmatively and constructing the category of categories as a subtype
{{\mathsf{Cat}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}
and verifying its essential properties.

1.1.
Directed univalence and
{\mathsf{Cat}}

What criteria should be used to determine if a subtype
{{\mathsf{Cat}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}
is a valid definition
of the category of categories? If one is not considering
\infty
-categories, the answer to this
question is straightforward:
{\mathsf{Cat}}
is a valid definition if the objects denote precisely small
types satisfying
\mathsf{isCat}
, synthetic morphisms are exactly functions (i.e., functors) between these
types, and the composition and identity operations behave as expected. In the
\infty
-categorical
case, the story does not end here; we must also convince ourselves that all the higher synthetic
morphisms also behave “as expected”. However, it is far from clear what the expected behavior
ought to be! Instead, we take a different approach by isolating a particular universal property
for the embedding
{\mathsf{Cat}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{U}
and arguing that all the other properties of
{\mathsf{Cat}}

stem from this single property.

The universal property in question is an
\infty
-categorical version of the Grothendieck
correspondence, often referred to as straightening–unstraightening in the
\infty
-categorical
context. Classically, the Grothendieck correspondence states there is an equivalence between
pseudofunctors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathbf{Cat}
for some 1-category
\mathcal{C}
and pairs of a category

\mathcal{E}
and a cocartesian family
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{C}
.
2
2

2

In the 1-
and 2-categorical literature these are often referred to as Grothendieck opfibrations. We
use “cocartesian” as it is the standard term in
\infty
-category theory.
Naively, this suggests a
simple universal property for the type
{\mathsf{Cat}}
: We should require for every
\infty
-category
C

an equivalence between the type of functions
C\to{\mathsf{Cat}}
and the type

\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{E:\mathcal{U}}}{\textstyle\sum_{E:\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{E:\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{E:\mathcal{U}}}\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{\pi:E\to C}}{\textstyle\sum_{\pi:E\to C}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{\pi:E\to C}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{\pi:E\to C}}\IsCocart\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\pi$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\pi$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\pi$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\pi$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Unfortunately, this definition is too
straightforward: if such a
{\mathsf{Cat}}
existed we would be able to show that
\IsCocart
held for every
map
\pi
which is certainly not true.

The problem is familiar to cubical type theorists: we are essentially asking for
{\mathsf{Cat}}
to
be a
universal fibration
, where
\IsCocart
is our notion of fibrancy. It is well-known that
one cannot give universal property for the univalent universe in the extensional type theory of cubical sets
(
Orton and Pitts 2018
)
: the naive equivalence is only valid with respect to
closed

elements and cannot be internalized this way into type theory where it would also apply to elements
in an arbitrary context. A solution to this problem in cubical type theory was presented
by
Licata et al. 2018
. There the authors give a description of a universal fibration in an extensional type theory for cubical sets using
modal
type theory. They supplement type theory with an idempotent
comonad
\flat
, such that the modal type
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\flat\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\flat\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\flat\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\flat\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
contains only the
global
elements
of
A
. After further extending type theory with a right adjoint to
\mathbb{I}\to-
, they are then not only able to express the desired universal property (appropriately annotated with
\flat
), but
also use the aforementioned right adjoint to derive it.

This idea has been adapted to variants of simplicial type theory first by
Weaver and Licata 2020
and
later by
Gratzer et al. 2024
to construct a category of
discrete

\infty
-categories
(spaces). We follow on this line of work—specifically
Gratzer et al. 2024
—and use the
\flat

modality to state the correct universal property for the category of categories and produce a type
satisfying it. In particular:

{restatable*}
theoremunivcocart

{\mathsf{Cat}}
is the base of the universal cocartesian family, i.e., for any
C\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}
, we
have
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\flat\mid C\to{\mathsf{Cat}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\flat\mid C\to{\mathsf{Cat}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\flat\mid C\to{\mathsf{Cat}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\flat\mid C\to{\mathsf{Cat}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\simeq\mathchoice{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\langle}}\hbox{$\displaystyle\flat\mid\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}{\textstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}\IsCocart\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\rangle}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\langle}}\hbox{$\textstyle\flat\mid\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}{\textstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}\IsCocart\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\rangle}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\langle}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\flat\mid\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}{\textstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}\IsCocart\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\rangle}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\big\langle}{\langle}}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\flat\mid\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}{\textstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{E:C\to\mathcal{U}}}\IsCocart\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle E$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\big\rangle}{\rangle}}}
.

On its own, this statement is not worth much. We do not know whether
{\mathsf{Cat}}
is a category itself,
let alone to what its objects and morphisms correspond. To resolve this, we give a detailed analysis
of the structure of cocartesian fibrations over certain categories, relying on the established
results in simplicial type theory. As a particular case of these results, we conclude that
cocartesian fibrations over
\mathbb{I}
are determined by (1) a pair of categories
C
,
D
along with (2)
a function
C\to D
. Consequently, we are able to prove the directed univalence theorem for
{\mathsf{Cat}}
:

{restatable*}
[Directed univalence]corollarydua
If
A,B
are
\flat
-elements of
{\mathsf{Cat}}
, then there is a equivalence

\mathsf{dua}:\hom_{{\mathsf{Cat}}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}\simeq\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\flat\mid A\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\flat\mid A\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\flat\mid A\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\flat\mid A\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
.

Here we see the first major divergence between the case of categories and the previously
considered case of discrete categories. Directed univalence for
{\mathsf{Cat}}
only guarantees that

\hom_{{\mathsf{Cat}}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is equivalent to
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\flat\mid A\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\flat\mid A\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\flat\mid A\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\flat\mid A\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
whereas in the discrete case one
obtains an equivalence with
A\to B
. This is inevitable:
no
category
can have hom-spaces equivalent to
A\to B
for arbitrary categories
A,B
. However, the
appearance of modalities in directed univalence is a significant complication.

Further consequences of our analysis prove that
{\mathsf{Cat}}
is a category and that composition of
synthetic morphisms corresponds (under directed univalence) to composition of ordinary
functions. In total then, we show that the universal property of
{\mathsf{Cat}}
is sufficient to derive all
the expected behavior of a category of categories as well as uniquely characterize
{\mathsf{Cat}}
itself.

Directed univalence opens up a wide array of applications. For instance, we use it to show that

{\mathsf{Cat}}
contains a number of recognizable
reflective
subcategories (truncated categories,
partial orders, spaces, etc.). It also allows us to build new and important categories by
combining
{\mathsf{Cat}}
with existing type-theoretic connectives. As a simple example, we show that

\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{c:{\mathsf{Cat}}}}{\textstyle\sum_{c:{\mathsf{Cat}}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{c:{\mathsf{Cat}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{c:{\mathsf{Cat}}}}c
can be analyzed using directed univalence and use its attendant

structure homomorphism principle
(SHP) to see that
\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{c:{\mathsf{Cat}}}}{\textstyle\sum_{c:{\mathsf{Cat}}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{c:{\mathsf{Cat}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{c:{\mathsf{Cat}}}}c
is the
lax

slice category
\mathbf{1}\mathbin{/\kern-3.0pt/}{\mathsf{Cat}}
.

Finally, while
Section

1.1
is a form of the Grothendieck correspondence, it is not
the strongest form possible. This would state that there is an equivalence of

\infty
-categories
between cocartesian families over
C
and the functor category

C\to{\mathsf{Cat}}
. We adapt an argument sketched by
Cisinski et al. 2025
to
STT
to show that

Section

1.1
upgrades to an equivalence of categories and thereby give
a synthetic proof of the
straightening-unstraightening
theorem
(
Lurie 2009
)
.

1.2.
Contributions and outline

Our main contribution is the first construction of a directed univalent category of categories
within
STT
together with a verification of all its essential properties. This is the last major
primitive missing from the theory of
\infty
-categories in simplicial type theory and,
additionally, also constitutes a new and novel approach to a foundational theorem in

\infty
-category theory itself. With this in place, we also note that a putative full directed type
theory can be interpreted into
\infty
-categories by giving a mere syntactic translation to the
appropriate fragment of
STT
.

•

We extend the methodology of
Licata et al. 2018
to construct a universal cocartesian family

{\mathsf{Cat}}_{\bullet}\to{\mathsf{Cat}}
.

•

We derive purely from this universal property that
{\mathsf{Cat}}
is a category, satisfies directed
univalence, etc.

•

We prove various properties of
{\mathsf{Cat}}
and showcase the novel applications of SHP it enables.

•

Finally, we give a fully synthetic proof of the foundational straightening–unstraightening
theorem.

Over all, we demonstrate how type theory (through
STT
) is highly effective for proving major
results in higher category theory.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. In
Section

2
we recall the main
ideas of simplicial type theory, its triangulated type theory variant, and the modal extensions
thereof. This, along with
Section

3
on cocartesian fibrations, is intended to make the paper as
self-contained as possible. In
Section

4
we define
{\mathsf{Cat}}
and prove
Section

1.1
,
the main construction of this paper. In
Section

5
, we combine our new results on cocartesian
fibrations along with the results of the prior section to prove
Section

1.1
along with the
fact that
{\mathsf{Cat}}
is a category. Finally, in
Sections

6
and
7
, we give a
number of consequences of our results, including a proof of straightening–unstraightening
(
Corollary

6.4
).

2.
Simplicial and triangulated type theory

We now turn to giving a more precise account of the flavor of simplicial type theory we shall use. There are two major modifications to the theory compared with the original system
studied by
Riehl and Shulman 2017
. First is that we supplement our theory with a collection of
modalities, including the
\flat
modality discussed in the introduction. We do this using

MTT
, a general purpose modal dependent type theory
(
Gratzer et al. 2020
)
. Secondly, the intended
model of
STT
is simplicial spaces, the
\infty
-categorical version of
\mathbf{PSh}(\Delta)
. However,
in order to leverage the techniques of
Licata et al. 2018
to define
{\mathsf{Cat}}
, we require a
postulated
amazing right adjoint
to
\mathbb{I}\to-
. Unfortunately, in the intended model of
simplicial spaces, such a right adjoint simply does not exist; the category of simplices does not
have products.

To circumvent this,
Gratzer et al. 2024
introduced a further relaxation of the modal variant of
STT
:

triangulated type theory

TT
\boxbslash
. The intended model of
TT
\boxbslash
is
Dedekind cubical
spaces,
rather than simplicial ones. Simplicial spaces embed into this intended model as a subtopos and the
original structure therefore remains. Concretely, this change involves relaxing the requirement that

\mathbb{I}
be totally ordered to merely asking it to be a bounded distributive lattice. In this
section, we recall the details of
TT
\boxbslash
relevant for this work and, in particular, describe the
modal extensions necessary to construct the category of categories.

2.1.
First steps in triangulated type theory

As mentioned in
Section

1
,
STT
and its relaxation
TT
\boxbslash
, extend homotopy type theory.
Accordingly, we begin by recalling the basic definitions and notations from homotopy type theory.
For a more complete account, see the
Univalent Foundations Program 2013
.

Homotopy type theory begins with intensional Martin-Löf type theory, complete with a hierarchy of universes,
\mathcal{U}_{0},\mathcal{U}_{1},\dots
, and
intensional identity types
a=_{A}b
. We use
\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod}{\textstyle\prod}{\scriptstyle\prod}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod}
and
\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum}{\textstyle\sum}{\scriptstyle\sum}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum}
for the standard dependent product and sum types of this type theory and
use
p_{!}
for the transport map
B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}
induced by
p:x=y
. The key extension of

HoTT
, the univalence axiom, governs the behavior of the intensional identity type of the
universe. In particular, this axiom relates equality in the universe to
equivalences
. See op. cit. for a detailed discussion of the type of equivalences
A\simeq B
; we only
note that it is a subtype of
A\to B
which, when
A=B
, includes the identity function.

{axiom}
[]
The canonical map
A=_{\mathcal{U}_{i}}B\to A\simeq B
sending
{\mathsf{refl}}
to
\mathsf{id}
is an
equivalence.

We shall also have use for a few other
HoTT
concepts. First, since univalence causes the

unicity of identity proofs
to fail rather spectacularly, so we isolate those types for which
this property still holds as (homotopy)
sets
. We will also use for the stronger properties of
being a (homotopy)
proposition
, or
contractible
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{isSet}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{a,b:A}}{\textstyle\prod_{a,b:A}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{a,b:A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{a,b:A}}\mathsf{isProp}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a=b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a=b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a=b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a=b$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{isProp}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{a,b:A}}{\textstyle\prod_{a,b:A}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{a,b:A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{a,b:A}}\mathsf{isContr}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a=b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a=b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a=b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a=b$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{isContr}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{a:A}}{\textstyle\sum_{a:A}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{a:A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{a:A}}\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{b:A}}{\textstyle\prod_{b:A}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{b:A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{b:A}}a=b

If
\phi:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}
is valued in propositions, we write
\mathcal{U}_{\phi}
for

\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\textstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}\phi\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Moreover, we use
\mathsf{HProp}
as condensed notation for

\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{isProp}}
.

Next, we require a few
higher inductive types
(HITs). These are inductive types
that postulate constructors not only of elements of the type, but also of elements of its identity
type. For instance, we shall use pushouts
A+_{B}C
and localizations as defined by

Rijke et al. 2020
. Since the models of HITs satisfying strict equations
is somewhat fraught, we only assume that our HITs satisfy propositional computation rules, i.e., that
they are homotopy-initial algebras.

We now turn to the first and most basic axiom of triangulated type theory, which
introduces the directed interval.

{axiom}
[]
There is a set
\mathbb{I}
equipped with the structure of a bounded distributive lattice

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle 0,1,\land,\lor$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle 0,1,\land,\lor$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0,1,\land,\lor$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0,1,\land,\lor$}\mathclose{}{)}}
such that
0\neq 1
.

Crucially, compared with
STT
we do not assume that
\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\textstyle\prod_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}i\leq j\lor j\leq i

holds. However, just as with the failure of UIP, we may isolate those types that “believe
i\leq j\lor j\leq i
” as
simplicial
types. These are types
A
such that the following
holds:

\mathsf{isSimp}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\textstyle\prod_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}\mathsf{isEquiv}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{const}:A\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{const}:A\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{const}:A\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{const}:A\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i\leq j\lor j\leq i\to A$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

The next result follows from
Rijke et al. 2020
:

Proposition 2.1.

There is a lex idempotent monad
\boxbslash:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}
with
\boxbslash A
universal
among simplicial types receiving a map from
A
.

In other words, ordinary simplicial type theory is the “subset of
TT
\boxbslash
” given by types for which

\eta_{A}:A\to\boxbslash A
is an equivalence. We write
\mathcal{U}_{\boxbslash}=\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\textstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{A:\mathcal{U}}}\mathsf{isEquiv}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\eta_{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\eta_{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\eta_{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\eta_{A}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
for
the subtype of
\mathcal{U}
spanned by simplicial types and note that
\mathcal{U}_{\boxbslash}
is itself simplicial.
Consequently, there is a unique
\hat{\boxbslash}:\boxbslash\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}_{\boxbslash}
such that

\hat{\boxbslash}\circ\eta=\boxbslash
.

With
\mathbb{I}
, we can make a number of important definitions that isolate those types that actually
behave like categories. First, as mentioned earlier, we can define a
synthetic morphism

in a type
A
to be a map
\mathbb{I}\to A
. Constraining the endpoints of this map, we arrive at the
definition of the synthetic hom-type:

\hom_{A}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{f:\mathbb{I}\to A}}{\textstyle\sum_{f:\mathbb{I}\to A}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{f:\mathbb{I}\to A}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{f:\mathbb{I}\to A}}f\,0=a\times f\,1=b

Every type comes equipped with a collection of identity morphisms:
\mathsf{id}_{a}=\lambda\_.\,a:\hom\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,a$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. However, the other operation we expect from a category—composition—is not available
by default. What is available instead is a proof-relevant composition
relation
, stating that
a pair of synthetic morphisms compose to a third. To define these, we derive the
2
-simplex

\Delta^{2}
from
\mathbb{I}
. For the sake of future use, we define the
n
-simplex for any
n\geq 0
:

\Delta^{n}=\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{\vec{i}:\mathbb{I}^{n}}}{\textstyle\sum_{\vec{i}:\mathbb{I}^{n}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{\vec{i}:\mathbb{I}^{n}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{\vec{i}:\mathbb{I}^{n}}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i_{0}\geq i_{1}\geq\dots\geq i_{n-1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i_{0}\geq i_{1}\geq\dots\geq i_{n-1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i_{0}\geq i_{1}\geq\dots\geq i_{n-1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i_{0}\geq i_{1}\geq\dots\geq i_{n-1}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

Crucially, the 2-simplex can be visualized as a triangle (the shaded region below), if
\mathbb{I}
is
seen as a line segment whence
\mathbb{I}^{2}
as a square:

i
j
(0,0)
(1,0)
(0,1)
(1,1)

We also have need for the inner horn
\Lambda^{2}_{1}=\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\textstyle\sum_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}i=1\lor j=0
, a
subtype of
\Delta^{2}
which captures the two highlighted segments above.

Say that
\tau:\Delta^{2}\to A
is a witness for the fact that
\tau\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle-,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle-,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle-,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-,0$}\mathclose{}{)}},\tau\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle 1,-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle 1,-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1,-$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1,-$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\mathbb{I}\to A
compose to
\lambda i.\,\tau\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle i,i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle i,i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle i,i$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle i,i$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. A Segal type (sometimes called a pre-category)
is one where every pair of composable arrows admits a unique composition witness. To define this
formally, we note that maps
\Lambda^{2}_{1}\to A
precisely capture the data of composable
arrows:

Definition 2.2.

A type
A
is
Segal
if
\mathsf{isEquiv}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle A^{\Delta^{2}}\to A^{\Lambda^{2}_{1}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle A^{\Delta^{2}}\to A^{\Lambda^{2}_{1}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A^{\Delta^{2}}\to A^{\Lambda^{2}_{1}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A^{\Delta^{2}}\to A^{\Lambda^{2}_{1}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
holds.

{notation}

If
f,g:A^{\mathbb{I}}
and
p:f\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}\mathclose{}{)}}=g\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, we write
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle f,g,p$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle f,g,p$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f,g,p$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f,g,p$}\mathclose{}{]}}
for the induced map

A^{\Lambda^{2}_{1}}
and, if
A
is Segal,
g\circ_{p}f
for the composite. Furthermore, we shall
subsequently have use for the outer horns
\Lambda^{2}_{0}=\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\textstyle\sum_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}i=j\lor j=0
and

\Lambda^{2}_{2}=\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\textstyle\sum_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{i,j:\mathbb{I}}}i=1\lor i=j
. Finally, we write
\bar{i}
for the element of

\Delta^{n+i}
given by
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle 1,\dots 1,0,\dots$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle 1,\dots 1,0,\dots$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle 1,\dots 1,0,\dots$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle 1,\dots 1,0,\dots$}\mathclose{}{)}}
of
i
copies of
1
followed by
n
copies
of
0
.

Segal types enjoy a unique composition operation given by the inverse of the map

A^{\Delta^{2}}\to A^{\Lambda^{2}_{1}}
, and calculation shows that the aforementioned definition of the
identity morphism is a left and right unit for composition. However, objects in a pre-category have
two distinct notions of sameness: via either the identity type
or
synthetic isomorphism. By the
latter, we mean a morphism
f:\hom\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,b$}\mathclose{}{)}}
equipped with
g,h:\hom\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle b,a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle b,a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle b,a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle b,a$}\mathclose{}{)}}
along with composition
witnesses showing that
g
(
h
) is left (right) inverse to
f
. One can define

\mathbb{E}=\Delta^{2}\mathrel{\textstyle\sqcup_{\Lambda^{2}_{2}}}\mathbb{I}\mathrel{\textstyle\sqcup_{\Lambda^{2}_{0}}}\Delta^{2}
such that

\mathbb{E}\to X
precisely corresponds to an equivalence in
X

(
Buchholtz and Weinberger 2023
, §4.2)
). A
distinctive feature of
\infty
-category theory is that these two notions (object equality and
isomorphism) can be made to coincide; a property similar to the univalence axiom. We therefore also
single out those types which satisfy this local univalence condition:

Definition 2.3.

A type
A
is
Rezk
if
\mathsf{isEquiv}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{const}:A\to A^{\mathbb{E}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{const}:A\to A^{\mathbb{E}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{const}:A\to A^{\mathbb{E}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{const}:A\to A^{\mathbb{E}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Definition 2.4.

A simplicial, Segal, and Rezk type is called a
category
. A category whose
morphisms are all invertible is a
groupoid
.

{remark}

The general results of
Rijke et al. 2020
show that categories and groupoids are modal types for
idempotent monads. We write
\mathop{\bigcirc_{\mathsf{grpd}}}
for the idempotent modality associated with groupoids
in particular, i.e., nullification at
\mathbb{I}
.

We shall also have occasion to use the
relative
versions of the Segal and Rezk conditions.
Given a family of types
A:X\to\mathcal{U}
, we say that
A
is (
right
)
orthogonal
to a map
I\to J
if the following canonical map is an equivalence:

\mathchoice{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}^{J}\to X^{J}\times_{X^{I}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\textstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{x:X}}A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}^{I}

The relative Segal condition asks that a family of types
A:X\to\mathcal{U}
be right orthogonal to

\Lambda^{2}_{1}\to\Delta^{2}
and the relative Rezk condition asks the same for

\mathbb{E}\to\mathbf{1}
. A Segal family is called
inner
and a family that is both Segal and Rezk
is
iso-inner
.

For use in
Section

4
, we note that we can phrase the requirement that a family be
inner using the following predicate:

\displaystyle\mathsf{isInner}:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta^{2}\to\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\Delta^{2}\to\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Delta^{2}\to\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta^{2}\to\mathcal{U}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{HProp}

\displaystyle\mathsf{isInner}\,A=\mathsf{isEquiv}\mathchoice{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}A\,t$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}A\,t$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}A\,t$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}A\,t$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}A\,t$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}A\,t$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}} Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}A\,t$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}A\,t$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Lambda^{2}_{1}}}A\,t$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}A\,t$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}A\,t$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}A\,t$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}{\mathopen{\mathchoice{\big(}{\big(}{\big(}{(}}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\textstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{t:\Delta^{2}}}A\,t$}\mathclose{\mathchoice{\big)}{\big)}{\big)}{)}}}\to\mathchoice{\m A family
A\mathrel{:}X\to\mathcal{U}
is inner if and only if
\mathchoice{\textstyle\prod_{h:\Delta^{2}\to X}}{\textstyle\prod_{h:\Delta^{2}\to X}}{\scriptstyle\prod_{h:\Delta^{2}\to X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\prod_{h:\Delta^{2}\to X}}\mathsf{isInner}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle A\circ h$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle A\circ h$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A\circ h$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A\circ h$}\mathclose{}{)}}

holds.

{notation}

We shall often identify a family
A\mathrel{:}X\to\mathcal{U}
with its associated total
space projection
\pi
from
\widetilde{A}\coloneq\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{x\mathrel{:}X}}{\textstyle\sum_{x\mathrel{:}X}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{x\mathrel{:}X}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{x\mathrel{:}X}}A\,x
to
X
.
We shall say that an arbitrary map of types
f:X\to Y
is, for instance, inner if the
associated map
Y\to\mathcal{U}
sending
y
to
f^{-1}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is inner.

Given a family
A\mathrel{:}X\to\mathcal{U}
and an arrow
f\mathrel{:}\mathbb{I}\to X
we define

dependent arrows
over
f
from
a\mathrel{:}A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle f\,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle f\,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f\,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f\,0$}\mathclose{}{)}}
to

a^{\prime}\mathrel{:}A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle f\,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle f\,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f\,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f\,1$}\mathclose{}{)}}
as follows:

\hom_{f}^{A}(a,a^{\prime})\coloneq\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{\alpha\mathrel{:}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to A(f\,t)}}{\textstyle\sum_{\alpha\mathrel{:}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to A(f\,t)}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{\alpha\mathrel{:}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to A(f\,t)}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{\alpha\mathrel{:}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle t:\mathbb{I}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to A(f\,t)}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha\,0=a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\alpha\,0=a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\alpha\,0=a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha\,0=a$}\mathclose{}{)}}\times\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\alpha\,1=a^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\alpha\,1=a^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\alpha\,1=a^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha\,1=a^{\prime}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

In an inner family there exists an induced composition operation for dependent arrows
(
Buchholtz and Weinberger 2023
)
.

2.2.
Multimodal type theory

The next step in
TT
\boxbslash
is to include modalities: special type constructors that violate key
properties we ordinarily require in type theory, such as stability under substitution. We use
these modalities to internalize crucial operations from our intended model of cubical spaces such as
the discrete and codiscrete endofunctors, the opposite functor, etc. To this end, we recall
some of the details of the modal extension to type theory,
MTT
, following
(
Gratzer et al. 2020
)
. See
Gratzer 2023
for a more detailed account. Since our primary
goal is to write programs in
MTT
, we focus on the “informal” version of the syntax and
defer the formal rules (replete with de Bruijn indices and a substitution calculus) to

Appendix

A
.

First,
MTT
is parameterized by a
mode theory

\mathcal{M}
. This is a strict 2-category describing
the modalities (as 1-cells) and transformations between them (as 2-cells). While
MTT

also permits distinct type theories to be related by modalities by considering mode theories with
multiple modes (0-cells), we do not need this generality and therefore assume that mode theories
have only one object. We shall also only be concerned with mode theories with at most one 2-cell
between every pair of 1-cells i.e., 2-categories that are merely poset-enriched. As such, our mode theories are simply given by ordered monoids. The mode theory
required for
TT
\boxbslash
is described in
Section

2.3
, but we continue with an
arbitrary mode theory satisfying these constraints for the moment.

The main extension of
MTT
is to add a new modal type
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
for each modality
\mu\in\mathsf{Arr}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. However, as already mentioned modal types are somewhat peculiar, and to
accommodate them
MTT
also modifies context extension. Specifically, each variable in an
MTT

context is annotated with a “formal division” of modalities
x\mathrel{:_{\mu/\nu}}A
. We write

\Gamma/\nu
for the operation which modifies each annotation in
\Gamma
to send

x\mathrel{:_{\mu/\nu\/_{0}}}A
to
x\mathrel{:_{\mu/\nu\/_{0}\circ\nu}}A
. The variable rule is then
modified to account for these formal divisions as follows:

\frac{\mu\leq\nu\quad x\mathrel{:_{\mu/\nu}}A\in\Gamma}{\Gamma\vdash x:A}

Note that one can recover the ordinary rules for variables by considering the annotation

\mathsf{id}/\mathsf{id}
. As a matter of notation therefore, we generally suppress division by
\mathsf{id}

and omit the annotation entirely for
\mathsf{id}/\mathsf{id}
so that we instead write

x\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A
or
x\mathrel{:}A
.

These annotations are then manipulated by the modal operators
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
. In particular,
they are added by the formation and introduction rules. The (somewhat lengthy) elimination rule, on
the other hand, papers over the difference between annotations on a variable and modal types by
allowing us to convert a binding
x\mathrel{:_{\nu/\mathsf{id}}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
into a binding

y\mathrel{:_{\nu\circ\mu/\mathsf{id}}}A
:

\frac{\Gamma/\mu\vdash A\,\mathsf{type}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\,\mathsf{type}}\frac{\Gamma/\mu\vdash a:A}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}\\
\frac{\Gamma/\nu\circ\mu\vdash A\,\mathsf{type}\quad\Gamma,y\mathrel{:_{\nu/\mathsf{id}}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\vdash B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle y$}\mathclose{}{)}}\,\mathsf{type}\quad\quad\Gamma,x\mathrel{:_{\nu\circ\mu/\mathsf{id}}}A\vdash b\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}:B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}/y$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}/y$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}/y$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}/y$}\mathclose{}{]}}\quad\Gamma/\nu\vdash a:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}_{\nu}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}\leftarrow a\ \mathsf{in}\ b\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}:B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle a/y$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle a/y$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a/y$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a/y$}\mathclose{}{]}}}\mathsf{let}_{\nu}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}\leftarrow\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\ \mathsf{in}\ b\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}=b\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle a_{0}/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle a_{0}/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a_{0}/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a_{0}/x$}\mathclose{}{]}}

Aside from these modal types,
MTT
extends type theory with an extended version of the dependent
product type,
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}
. Up to equivalence, these modal dependent
products are the same as
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a\mathrel{:}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a\mathrel{:}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a\mathrel{:}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a\mathrel{:}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathsf{let}\ \mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\leftarrow a\ \mathsf{in}\ B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a_{0}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, but offer lighter-weight notation and support a stronger

\eta
-rule.

\frac{\Gamma/\mu\vdash A\,\mathsf{type}\quad\Gamma,a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A\vdash b\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}:B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash f:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}:B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}}

When we write “for all
a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A
, the type
B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is inhabited”
this should be understood to denote a function
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to B\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.

Finally, we require the following technical axiom on
MTT
which governs the interaction between
modalities and identity types:

{axiom}
[]

If
A\mathrel{:_{\mu}}\mathcal{U}
and
a,b\mathrel{:_{\mu}}A
, then the following canonical map sending

{\mathsf{refl}}
to
\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\mathsf{refl}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{refl}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{refl}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{refl}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is an equivalence:

\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle b$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle b$}\mathclose{}{)}}\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid a=b$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid a=b$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid a=b$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid a=b$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

We summarize several basic properties of modalities:

Proposition 2.5.

•

Each
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
commutes with
\mathbf{1}
and pullbacks.

•

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\simeq\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\circ\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\circ\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\circ\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\circ\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
and

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathsf{id}\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathsf{id}\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{id}\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{id}\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\simeq-
.

•

If
\mu\leq\nu
, then there is a canonical map

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
.

•

There is an equivalence

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\flat\mid A\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\sharp\mid B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\sharp\mid B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sharp\mid B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\sharp\mid B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\flat\mid A\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\sharp\mid B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\sharp\mid B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\flat\mid A\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\sharp\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\flat\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}

for
A,B\mathrel{:_{\flat}}\mathcal{U}
. Similarly with
\sharp
and
\flat
replaced with
\mathrm{op}
.

From the first point, we obtain

\mathbin{\circledast}:\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\times\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid A$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}\to\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mid B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mid B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mid B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mid B$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}
.
We often write
f^{\dagger}
for
\mathsf{mod}_{\mu}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle f$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle f$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mathbin{\circledast}-
, when the latter is
well-formed.

2.3.
Modalities in triangulated type theory

We now turn from
MTT
generally to the specific instantiation of
MTT
we require for
TT
\boxbslash
.
Namely, we will work with the mode theory generated by one 0-cell
m
, three generating non-identity
1-cells
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\flat,\sharp,\mathrm{op}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\textstyle\flat,\sharp,\mathrm{op}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\flat,\sharp,\mathrm{op}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}{\mathopen{}{\{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\flat,\sharp,\mathrm{op}$}\mathclose{}{\}}}
, and the following (in)equalities:

\flat=\flat\circ\flat=\flat\circ\sharp=\flat\circ\mathrm{op}\sharp=\sharp\circ\flat=\sharp\circ\sharp=\sharp\circ\mathrm{op}\flat\leq\mathsf{id}=\mathrm{op}\circ\mathrm{op}\leq\sharp

For intuition, in the cubical spaces model of
TT
\boxbslash
the modal operations
for the generating modalities are interpreted as follows:

•

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\displaystyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\textstyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}{\mathopen{}{\langle}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\flat\mid-$}\mathclose{}{\rangle}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}
